{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-7.34723pt}{2.49306pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{2.93054pt}{2.25pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{}}{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{}{}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\lxSVG@setdash{0.8pt,2.0pt}{0.0pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-dasharray,stroke-dashoffset} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 27.67 0 C 11.07 27.67 -11.07 27.67 -27.67 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
\lxSVG@closescope
\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{}}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\,\,,

(120)

\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{i^{q-2}J^{2}}{2^{q-2}}\Bigg[\sum_{1\leqslant k<q,\text{odd}}{q-1\choose k-1}G_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})^{q-k}\left(G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})^{k-1}+G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})^{k-1}-\delta_{k,1}\right)\Bigg]

(121)

where the last term is to compensate the double counting in the summation when
k=1
. For later convenience, we may rewrite

G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})^{k-1}+G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})^{k-1}=(G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2}))^{k-1}\,,\quad\text{(for odd $k>1$)}

(122)

since the mixed terms all vanish due to time constraints for the retarded and advanced Green functions. Again, the
k=1
term needs individual care, and we find

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{i^{q-2}J^{2}}{2^{q-1}}

\displaystyle\left[\left(G_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})+G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}\right.

(123)

\displaystyle\left.+\left(G_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})+G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}\right]\,.

Note that the retarded and advanced Green functions
G^{{\mathrm{R}},{\mathrm{A}}}_{\alpha\beta}
are diagonal in the fold index (i.e. vanish if
\alpha\not=\beta
); therefore the corresponding self-energies
\Sigma^{{\mathrm{R}},{\mathrm{A}}}_{\alpha\beta}
are also diagonal. In contrast, the Keldysh component
\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}_{\alpha\beta}
is not necessarily diagonal.

An alternative way to obtain the above relations is to work in the
(u,d)
basis, where the vertex is diagonal

\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma^{uu}&\Sigma^{ud}\\
\Sigma^{du}&\Sigma^{dd}\end{pmatrix}=iJ^{2}\begin{pmatrix}-(iG^{uu})^{q-1}&(iG^{ud})^{q-1}\\
(iG^{du})^{q-1}&-(iG^{dd})^{q-1}\end{pmatrix}\,.

(124)

Besides the basis transformation law of
G
shown in (
101
), one needs to use a similar rule for
\Sigma
:

\begin{pmatrix}0&\Sigma^{\mathrm{A}}\\
\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}&\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}\end{pmatrix}=\frac{1}{2}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Sigma^{uu}&\Sigma^{ud}\\
\Sigma^{du}&\Sigma^{dd}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\
1&-1\end{pmatrix}\,.

(125)

C.5
Quasiparticle decay rate
\Gamma
for SYK at weak coupling

As a simple application of the formalism, we will estimate the quasiparticle decay rate
\Gamma
for the SYK model at
\beta J\ll 1
. This quantity is defined by the
t\to\infty
asymptotics of the Green function,
G^{{\mathrm{R}}}(t,0)\sim e^{-\Gamma t/2}
. Let us make a stronger assumption and adopt the ansatz
\widetilde{G}^{\mathrm{R}}(\omega)\approx\frac{1}{\omega+i\Gamma/2}
, which is only qualitatively correct. The task is to self-consistently determine
\Gamma
using the equations for the Green function and self-energy. First, by definition,

A(\omega)=-2\operatorname{Im}\widetilde{G}^{\mathrm{R}}(\omega)\approx\frac{\Gamma}{\omega^{2}+\Gamma^{2}/4}\,,\quad\widetilde{G}^{{\mathrm{K}}}(\omega)=-i\frac{1-e^{-\beta\omega}}{1+e^{-\beta\omega}}A(\omega)\,.

(126)

For a large
t
, we have

G^{\mathrm{R}}(t,0)\approx-i\theta(t)e^{-\Gamma t/2}\,,\quad G^{\mathrm{K}}(t,0)\approx-i\frac{1-e^{i\Gamma\beta/2}}{1+e^{i\Gamma\beta/2}}e^{-\Gamma|t|/2}\,.

(127)

Inserting these expressions into (
117
), we obtain an explicit formula for the retarded self-energy:

\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}(t,0)\approx-i\frac{J^{2}}{2^{q-2}}\theta(t)e^{-(q-1)\Gamma t/2}\underbrace{\sum_{1\leqslant k<q,{\rm odd}}{q-1\choose k-1}x^{k-1}}_{=\frac{1}{2}\left[(1+x)^{q-1}+(1-x)^{q-1}\right]}\,,\quad x=\frac{1-e^{i\Gamma\beta/2}}{1+e^{i\Gamma\beta/2}}

(128)

We are interested in the high temperature limit, where
x\approx-i\frac{\Gamma\beta}{4}
is small, and therefore, negligible for the leading order calculation. (In other words, the diagonal component of the Keldysh function is small,
|G^{\mathrm{K}}|\ll|G^{\mathrm{R}}|
.) Next, we estimate the zero frequency value of the self-energy by integrating its long-time asymptotic form (
128
),
8
8

8

This is a crude approximation, and we will comment on its limitations shortly.
i.e.

\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{R}}(0)\approx-i\frac{J^{2}}{(q-1)2^{q-3}}\frac{1}{\Gamma}\,.

(129)

This quantity should be set to
-i\Gamma/2
as a self-consistency condition. Thus, we obtain

\Gamma\approx\frac{1}{\sqrt{q-1}\,2^{q/2-2}}\,J\,.

(130)

We now make a few remarks about this formula.

1.

The quasiparticle decay rate
\Gamma
is of order
J
instead of
J^{2}
; the latter is what one might have guessed based on perturbation theory as all disorder-averaged diagrams come with even powers of
J
. However, the naive perturbative argument fails due to the divergence arising from the long-lived bare Green function. We need to include the quasiparticle decay in the first place. On the other hand,
\Gamma\sim J
is a reasonable result from the dimensional analysis perspective since
J
is the only energy scale at infinite temperature.

2.

We should not trust the order
1
prefactor because in (
129
), we have used the long-time asymptotics to approximate
\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}(t)
. It is not valid at times
t\lesssim 1/\Gamma
, and therefore, the expression for
\Gamma
is off by an order
1
factor, though the
J
scaling is correct. We expect the approximation (
129
) and the final formula to become accurate in the
q\rightarrow 2
limit. Indeed, as shown in Fig.
3
, the approximate formula (
130
) agrees with the numerical solution for
q=2
.

3.

We can also compare (
130
) with the exact large
q
result (see Appendix
F
), which we denote by
\Gamma_{*}
. Specifically,
\Gamma_{*}=2vq^{-1}
and
v\approx 2^{3/2-q/2}\sqrt{q}J
for weak coupling. There is some discrepancy, with
\Gamma
given by (
130
) equal to
\Gamma_{*}/\sqrt{2}
in the
q\to\infty
limit. This is not a surprise for the reason discussed in point 2.

q
\Gamma/\Gamma_{*}
1
1/\sqrt{2}
2
4
6
8
10
12
14
16
18

Figure 3:
Quasiparticle decay rate
\Gamma
in units of
\Gamma_{*}=\sqrt{\frac{2^{5-q}}{q}}J
as a function of
q
. The red line,
\Gamma/\Gamma_{*}=1
, is the analytic large
q
result, whereas the blue line,
\Gamma/\Gamma_{*}=\sqrt{\frac{q}{2(q-1)}}
, represents the estimate (
130
). The black circles are numerical results for even integer
q
at
\beta=0
.

R(t)

(131)

where
a,b
label field flavors (not to be confused with fold indices
\alpha,\beta
). Here we assume that the flavors on the top and the bottom rails come in identical pairs. That is a nontrivial condition, restricting the class of models we can handle.

To formulate the problem, we consider the interaction vertex
\lambda_{abc}\psi_{a}^{\dagger}\psi_{b}O_{c}
, where
O_{c}
is a bosonic field that mediates the interaction between the rails (cf. Eq.
(5.1)
in Ref.
[
18
]
). Without loss of generality, we choose
O_{c}
to be real; namely, we treat the real and imaginary parts of a complex field separately. In the Keldysh basis
(u,d)
, the interaction is written as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=-i\lambda_{abc}\left[(\psi^{u}_{a})^{\dagger}\psi_{b}^{u}O_{c}^{u}-(\psi^{d}_{a})^{\dagger}\psi_{b}^{d}O_{c}^{d}\right]

(132)

\displaystyle\text{i.e.}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-19.66666pt}{3.533pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{3.533pt}{-20.15277pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

\lxSVG@closescope {{
{}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\right)\,.

(133)

In our formalism, only the third term in (
133
) will contribute to the rung function for the retarded kernel. Thus, we have

\displaystyle R_{ab}(t)=\sum_{cc^{\prime}}\lambda_{abc}\lambda_{bac^{\prime}}G_{cc^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{K}}(t)\,,

(134)

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad G_{cc^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{K}}(t)=\operatorname{Tr}\left(\sqrt{\rho}O_{c}(t)\sqrt{\rho}O_{c^{\prime}}(0)\right)\,,\quad\rho=e^{-\beta H}/Z\,.

(135)

To relate
R_{ab}
to
R^{*}_{ba}
, we will examine how each factor in (
134
) is transformed under complex conjugation. In fact,
G_{cc^{\prime}}^{\mathrm{K}}(t)
is real since
O_{c}
and
O_{c^{\prime}}
are real and the configuration we have chosen is symmetric on the imaginary time circle. As for the coefficients
\lambda_{abc}
, we will use the Hermiticity of the interaction Hamiltonian:

H_{\rm int}=\sum_{abc}\lambda_{abc}\psi^{\dagger}_{a}\psi_{b}O_{c}\,,\quad H_{\rm int}=H^{\dagger}_{\rm int}\quad\Rightarrow\quad\lambda_{abc}=\lambda_{bac}^{*}\,.

(136)

It follows that

\lambda_{abc}\lambda_{bac^{\prime}}=(\lambda_{bac}\lambda_{abc^{\prime}})^{*}\,.

(137)

Together with the reality of
G^{\mathrm{K}}
, this implies the desired identity,
R_{ab}(t)=R^{*}_{ba}(t)
.

Appendix E
Branching time for Brownian SYK

The Brownian SYK
[
27
,
28
]
and the regular SYK have the same form for Hamiltonian

H(t)=i^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{j_{1}<\ldots<j_{q}}J_{j_{1}...j_{q}}(t)\psi_{j_{1}}\ldots\psi_{j_{q}}\,,

(138)

but the random ensembles for the couplings are different:

\displaystyle\text{regular:}

\displaystyle\overline{J_{j_{1}...j_{q}}(t)J_{j^{\prime}_{1}...j^{\prime}_{q}}(t^{\prime})}=\delta_{j_{1}j_{1}^{\prime}}\ldots\delta_{j_{q}j_{q}^{\prime}}\frac{J^{2}(q-1)!}{N^{q-1}}\,,

(139)

\displaystyle\text{Brownian:}

\displaystyle\overline{J_{j_{1}...j_{q}}(t)J_{j^{\prime}_{1}...j^{\prime}_{q}}(t^{\prime})}=\delta_{j_{1}j_{1}^{\prime}}\ldots\delta_{j_{q}j_{q}^{\prime}}f(t-t^{\prime})\frac{J(q-1)!}{N^{q-1}}\,.

Here
f
is an even narrowly peaked function with integral equal to
1
, so that
f(t)\approx\delta(t)
for most purposes.

The retarded self-energy
\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}
has the following form for the Brownian SYK (by analogy with the regular SYK):

\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{i^{q+2}Jf(t_{1}-t_{2})}{2^{q-1}}\left[\left(G^{\mathrm{R}}(t_{1},t_{2})+G^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}+\left(G^{\mathrm{R}}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}\right]\,.

(140)

Since
f(t_{1}-t_{2})
enters as an overall factor, we may assume that
t_{1}-t_{2}
is small and use the UV asymptotics of
G^{\mathrm{R}}
and
G^{\mathrm{K}}
, namely,
G^{\mathrm{R}}(t)\approx-i\theta(t)
and
G^{\mathrm{K}}(t)\approx 0
. Thus,

\Sigma^{\mathrm{R}}(t)=\frac{-iJf(t)}{2^{q-2}}\theta(t)\approx\frac{-iJ}{2^{q-1}}\delta(t)\,.

(141)

This gives the quasiparticle decay rate,

\Gamma:=2i\widetilde{\Sigma}^{\mathrm{R}}(0)=\frac{J}{2^{q-2}}\,.

(142)

Now, the Keldysh self-energy
\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}
for the Brownian SYK is as follows:

\displaystyle\Sigma^{\mathrm{K}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})=\frac{i^{q+2}J\delta(t_{1}-t_{2})}{2^{q-1}}

\displaystyle\left[\left(G_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})+G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}\right.

(143)

\displaystyle\left.+\left(G_{\alpha\beta}^{\mathrm{K}}(t_{1},t_{2})-G^{\mathrm{R}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})+G^{\mathrm{A}}_{\alpha\beta}(t_{1},t_{2})\right)^{q-1}\right]\,.

For the calculation of OTOC and Lyapunov exponent, we are interested in off-diagonal matrix elements, e.g.
\alpha=1
,
\beta=2
:

\Sigma^{K}_{12}(t)=\frac{i^{q+2}J\delta(t)}{2^{q-2}}\left(G_{12}^{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right)^{q-1}\,.

(144)

The rung function,

R(t)=\frac{\delta\Sigma_{12}^{\mathrm{K}}}{\delta G_{12}^{K}}=(q-1)\frac{i^{q+2}J\delta(t)}{2^{q-2}}\left(G_{12}^{\mathrm{K}}(t)\right)^{q-2}

(145)

has a delta function factor, and therefore, is an example where the zero-range potential approximation is exact. Finally, we insert the UV form of
G^{\mathrm{K}}
,

G^{\mathrm{K}}_{12}(0)=2i\langle\psi\psi\rangle=i

(146)

into the formula for
R(t)
and get

R(t)=(q-1)\frac{J\delta(t)}{2^{q-2}}\,,\quad\widetilde{R}(0)=(q-1)\frac{J}{2^{q-2}}\,.

(147)

Thus, we obtain the Lyapunov exponent and the branching time:

\varkappa=\widetilde{R}(0)-\Gamma=(q-2)\frac{J}{2^{q-2}}\,,\quad t_{B}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{R}(0)}=\frac{2^{q-2}}{(q-1)J}\,.

(148)

The explicit form of the Lyapunov exponent is consistent with the expectation that for
q>2
, the model is chaotic. We have also checked the calculated Lyapunov exponent against the numerical result in
[
28
]
and found a good agreement.

Appendix F
Branching time for the regular SYK model at large
q

The large
q
SYK model was studied in
[
13
]
. Starting from the standard SYK Hamiltonian,

H=i^{\frac{q}{2}}\sum_{j_{1}\ldots<j_{q}}J_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}}\psi_{j_{1}}\ldots\psi_{j_{q}},\qquad\{\psi_{j},\psi_{k}\}=\delta_{jk},\qquad\overline{J^{2}_{j_{1},\ldots,j_{q}}}=\frac{J^{2}(q-1)!}{N^{q-1}}\,,

(149)

the order of limits is this: first take the
N\to\infty
limit, and then take
q
to infinity while keeping
\mathcal{J}=\sqrt{2^{1-q}q}\,J
fixed. In this limit, the two-point function can be computed for any
\mathcal{J}
:

G(\tau)=-\frac{\operatorname{sgn}(\tau)}{2}\left[\frac{\cos\frac{\pi v}{2}}{\cos\pi v\left(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{|\tau|}{\beta}\right)}\right]^{2\Delta},\qquad\text{where}\quad\frac{v}{2\cos\frac{\pi v}{2}}=\mathcal{J}\,,\quad\beta=2\pi.

(150)

The parameter
v\in(0,1)
characterizes the coupling strength.
At strong coupling,
\mathcal{J}\gg 1
and
v\approx 1-\frac{1}{\pi\mathcal{J}}
, whereas at weak coupling,
\mathcal{J}\ll 1
and
v\approx 2\mathcal{J}
. Roughly speaking,
v
determines the effective time scale in the system. For example, the quasiparticle decay rate at weak coupling is given by
\Gamma=2v\Delta
as indicated by the
\tau\to i\infty
asymptotics of the two-point function. We will also need the rung function,

R(t)=\frac{v^{2}}{2\cosh^{2}\frac{vt}{2}}\,,\qquad\widetilde{R}(\omega)=\frac{2\pi\omega}{\sinh(\pi\omega/v)}\,.

(151)

We now compare the exact expressions for the Lyapunov exponent
[
13
]
and branching time
[
18
]
,

\varkappa=v\,,\qquad t_{B}=\frac{3}{2v}\,,

(152)

with the ones derived from the zero-range potential approximation, see section
4.2
. Using the fact that
\Gamma
is small, we get

\varkappa_{\text{approx}}=\widetilde{R}(0)-\Gamma\approx 2v\,,\qquad t_{B,\text{approx}}=\frac{1}{\widetilde{R}(0)}=\frac{1}{2v}\,.

(153)

Note an order
1
factor discrepancy between the approximate and exact results. This discrepancy is expected since the “potential term”
R(t)=\frac{v^{2}}{2\cosh^{2}\frac{vt}{2}}
can not be well approximated by a delta function.
